5 Thriller Movies We Actually Want To See a Remake Of

📅 Nov 22, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Feel free to connect with him or check out his work. He's everywhere — Upwork, YouTube, Spotify, SoundCloud, Collider, LinkedIn, Instagram.

Remakes have a bad reputation, and unfortunately, most of the time, they have earned it. The problem is that studios love banking on famous titles and hoping nostalgia will do the heavy lifting. That doesn’t really work with the thriller genre, where the whole experience depends on not knowing what happens next. Once audiences know the twist, there is very little reason to sit through the same story again.

A straight copy of a classic thriller has almost no suspense left to offer. This means that the thrillers that are actually worth remaking are a little different. They’re the ones with brilliant setups that were either held back by their era or were way ahead of their time, with their fears feeling more real today than ever before. Here is a list of five thriller movies people actually want to see remade, as long as they have the right filmmakers behind them.

Dead Calm is a thriller that only needs three people and a lot of open water. The film follows Nicole Kidman and Sam Neill as Rae and John Ingram, a married couple who set out on a long sailing trip across the Pacific to grieve after their young son is killed in a car accident. Out in the middle of nowhere, they spot a man named Hughie (Billy Zane) frantically rowing away from a sinking ship. Hughie claims that the rest of his crew died of food poisoning, but when John rows over to investigate, the bodies he discovers tell a very different story. By then, Hughie has taken off in the couple’s yacht, and Rae is trapped aboard with him.

The best thing about Dead Calm is how simple it is. The film is a cat-and-mouse game with nowhere to run and nobody coming to help. What’s even better is that director Phillip Noyce squeezes every bit of tension out of this narrative. That complexity makes it a natural fit for a modern remake. A new version would just need to deal with the fact that satellite phones and GPS make getting stranded at sea much harder today, which could actually add an interesting twist to the story. All a Dead Calm remake needs is a smart director and two strong leads to become the kind of adrenaline-fueled, nerve-wracking thriller that has been missing from the big screen for a while now.

Brian De Palma’s Blow Out bombed at the box office when it first came out, but it has since been recognized as one of the greatest conspiracy thrillers ever made. The film stars John Travolta as sound-effects technician Jack Terry, who works on cheap slasher movies. One night, while recording sounds in a park, Jack captures a car swerving off a bridge and into the river. He manages to rescue a young woman named Sally (Nancy Allen) from the wreck, but the man driving, a governor who was planning to run for president, is killed. When Jack plays back his tape, he hears something that sounds like a gunshot right before the tire blows out, and he soon realizes he has recorded a murder that powerful people want covered up.

Blow Out is a thriller story about how easily the truth can be buried, and that idea feels even more relevant now. In 1981, Jack’s audio tape was the only proof that something was wrong. A modern remake of the film could explore what happens when evidence can be faked, edited, or dismissed as a deepfake in seconds. Jack’s obsession with proving what he heard would hit even harder in a world where nobody knows what to believe anymore. The original’s devastating ending is also a big part of why audiences rejected it back then, but it works perfectly today. Blow Out is a near-perfect film, but its central idea is begging to be updated for the age of misinformation.

Marathon Man is the movie that made an entire generation afraid of going to the dentist. The story follows history graduate Babe Levy (Dustin Hoffman), who spends his free time training for a marathon. His life is turned upside down when his older brother, Doc (Roy Scheider), turns out to be a secret government agent who has gotten tangled up with Dr. Christian Szell (Laurence Olivier), a Nazi war criminal hiding in South America. What’s worse is that Szell has come to New York to collect a fortune in stolen diamonds, and he is convinced that Babe knows something that could put them at risk. Marathon Man, written by William Goldman from his own novel, is famous for the scene where Szell tortures Babe with dental tools, along with the one where Holocaust survivors recognize him on the streets of New York’s diamond district.

Olivier’s menacing performance earned him an Oscar nomination for the role and practically defined the film. Hoffman also deserves his flowers for portraying Babe’s confusion with extreme conviction, which made the audience sympathize with him every step of the way. Nearly 50 years later, the idea of a war criminal hiding in plain sight, protected by money and powerful friends, still feels frighteningly relevant. Most younger viewers have never seen the original, though, and a fresh version could introduce them to one of the most unsettling stories the genre has ever produced.

The Parallax View is the ultimate paranoid thriller, and it might be even more unsettling today than it was in the 1970s. The film begins with the assassination of a U.S. senator at the top of Seattle’s Space Needle. Years later, reporter Joe Frady (Warren Beatty) notices that witnesses to the killing keep dying one after another. His investigation leads him to the Parallax Corporation, a shadowy company that seems to be recruiting troubled men and training them to become assassins.

The film, directed by Alan J. Pakula, who also made All the President’s Men, was released at a time when Americans had lost faith in the people in charge after a string of political assassinations and the Watergate scandal. Its most famous scene is a disturbing montage that Parallax uses to test its recruits, where people are shown flashing images of family and patriotism alongside violence until it’s hard to tell the difference. That idea of a company quietly searching for angry, lonely men to manipulate feels eerily close to how online radicalization works today. A remake could easily swap the old-fashioned test for social media algorithms, and it would be just as terrifying. Paramount+ announced plans for a series version back in 2021, but there has been little news since, and The Parallax View is long overdue for an update.

Wait Until Dark features one of the scariest showdowns in movie history, and it’s the perfect candidate for a remake. The film, based on Frederick Knott’s play, stars Audrey Hepburn as Susy Hendrix, a woman who recently lost her sight and lives in a New York City apartment with her husband, Sam (Efrem Zimbalist Jr.). Without realizing it, Sam brings home a doll that has been stuffed with heroin, and a group of criminals led by the ruthless Roat (Alan Arkin) will do anything to get it back. They come up with an elaborate con to trick Susy into handing it over, but it turns out she is not one to underestimate.

The film’s climax is what most people remember it for. Susy realizes that if she can’t see, the best way to win is to make sure nobody else can either, and she smashes every light in the apartment. When the film first came out, some theaters even dimmed their lights to make the sequence feel even scarier. The problem is that much of the first half has aged poorly, since almost everyone around Susy treats her like a helpless child. A remake could keep the brilliant premise while giving Susy the strength and intelligence she deserves from the very first scene. Hepburn earned an Oscar nomination for the role, but Wait Until Dark is ready for a brand-new heroine who can outsmart her attackers right from the start.

Battlestar Galactica’s Official Return Is A Big Hit With Gamers

📅 Nov 22, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Battlestar Galactica really does seem to be coming back to the fore after a number of years in stasis, where fans were serviced with very few true worthwhile adaptations. While things remain reasonably quiet on the television and film front, things are really popping off elsewhere, particularly in physical releases and the world of video games.

Master Replicas are continuing to produce statues of some of the more iconic ships in franchise history, and they have plans for plenty more besides. They've even produced renditions of ships that have previously been unfilmed, like the Cerberus, expanding the lore of the series in the process.

As for our medium of choice, there have also been recent video game releases that are set within the IP, including one in particular which has received solid feedback from fans to date.

Battlestar Galactica: Scattered Hopes is a survival fleet-management roguelite that features procedural narrative design and real-time space battles. It was released in May of this year, and it essentially tasks the player with making crucial decisions to keep their crew and ship going, all while pursued by the Cylon Fleet. The ultimate goal is to reconvene with the Battlestar Galactica, and it was very well-reviewed by critics when it debuted.

Happily, though the most recent reviews are more mixed, the general feedback on the game is positive from both longtime fans of the franchise, and those who were going into the experience blind.

"A very enjoyable fight against the inevitable roguelike. While I completed my first run and am a little worried about the meta progression making later runs too easy, I had a lot of fun. For much of the run I felt constantly like I was going to fail, and hit the exact vibe you want in a game like this of just eeking out a win," one review read.

While another added, "This is an impressive RTS with pause that covers both fleet management, missions, and combat. The tutorial is gripping, and stressful, and feels like a real mission rather than contrived situations to teach controls. I loved every minute of it, and this is a far more ‘realistic’ and enjoyable Battlestar Galactica game than some of the disastrous predecessors."

"A great roguelite that comes close to scratching that FTL itch! It’s challenging and occasionally repetitive, but rewarding, with new things to discover as you progress. What really stands out is the excellent BSG fan service. I love how the game interprets the Battlestar Galactica universe and captures its atmosphere, with plenty of little details for fans to appreciate. Highly recommended, especially for BSG fans!" a third said.

Battlestar Galactica: Scattered Hopes is out now and there's a free demo you can try out if you're not quite sold on making the full purchase just from the written pitch. The complete game has a standard base price of $24.99 USD, though it has been the subject of regular sales that take 50% off that price or more, likely making it well worth the outlay in the process.

‘American Psycho’ Gets Axed: Luca Guadagnino Confirms His Adaptation Is “Not Happening”

📅 Nov 22, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Luca Guadagnino has to return some videotapes. The Oscar-nominated director is passing on Scott Z. Burns‘ American Psycho adaptation.

While appearing on Deadline’s Crew Call podcast, Guadagnino told Anthony D’Allesandro that he is no longer attached to the projects, two years after we exclusively revealed he and Burns were adapting Bret Easton Ellis’ 1991 novel.

“Scott Z. Burns wrote a fantastic script,” said Guadagnino. “Unfortunately for me, as we speak, it’s not happening.”

In October 2024, Deadline revealed that Guadagnino was in final talks with Lionsgate to direct Burns’ adaptation, which would be produced by Frenesy Films, and executive produced by Sam Pressman, the son of Edward R. Pressman, producer of 2000 original movie, through his company Pressman Film. Watch on Deadline

Serving as a brief respite from the years-long casting hunt for the next James Bond, there was plenty of speculation about who would play Patrick Bateman. One actor to campaign for the role was Patrick Schwarzenegger, while Guadagnino’s Queer (2024) star Drew Starkey noted he read the “amazing” script.

But in February, Ellis claimed that multiple high-profile actors “have turned it down,” adding that Burns had since completed a new draft after the unnamed talent passed on the previous version.

Ellis teased that the film would be “completely different” from Mary Harron’s 2000 adaptation of American Psycho, which starred Christian Bale as homicidal investment banker Patrick Bateman, the literary character that Ellis created as a satirical take on masculinity. The Harron-helmed adaptation also starred Justin Theroux, Josh Lucas, Chloë Sevigny, Reese Witherspoon, Jared Leto, Willem Dafoe and more.

Anya Taylor-Joy’s Sci-Fi Thriller Is Still Her Greatest Movie 2 Years Later

📅 Nov 22, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Anya Taylor-Joy has brought so much to her many hits over the years, but her greatest movie is the one where she says the least. The 2024 prequel Furiosa: A Mad Max Saga sees her take over the title role from Charlize Theron and portray the early years of the post-apocalyptic warrior who became the highlight of 2015’s Mad Max: Fury Road. With George Miller’s direction and Taylor-Joy’s exceptional on-screen presence, the movie became an instant classic.

Set years before the events of Fury Road, in the radioactive wasteland of post-apocalyptic Australia, the film follows the path of Furiosa from childhood, where she is played by Alyla Browne, to early adulthood, where Taylor-Joy takes over. Stolen from her idyllic sanctuary home and forcibly adopted by warlord Dementus (Chris Hemsworth), she rises up the ranks of the Citadel, ruled by the tyrannical Immortan Joe (Lachy Hulme). She becomes caught in the middle of the rivalry between the two men, and begins using their ambition to exact revenge and work her way to freedom.

The movie is the fifth in the Mad Max franchise, but the first not to prominently feature the character of Max Rockatansky (who appears in a cameo played by Jacob Tomuri, Tom Hardy’s stunt double in Fury Road) — though many fans of the 2015 movie argue that Theron’s performance as Furiosa was the most compelling in the film, and that she was the story’s true lead. This prequel fulfills that potential with a captivating adventure worthy of its lofty predecessors.

Warning: you will cry over two different rocks.

Narratively speaking, Furiosa is an expansion of the world that was introduced in Fury Road. The 2015 film showed us the landscape passing by at 100 miles per hour, in what was essentially one big, beautiful chase movie. Max’s mission was simple – race to escape the Citadel, and then turn back to reclaim it. Furiosa’s origin story is more complex, divided into chapters and focused more on the characters involved. The torment she endures, and the loss that hardens her, are all essential to the plot, making for a much darker and more thoughtful movie.

Both are classics, but Miller’s approach is different each time. Fury Road is a practically perfect example of an action movie, with choreography and spectacle that has few equals before or since. Furiosa is an epic revenge tragedy, examining the harrowing path one must take to be compelled to overthrow tyranny. Furiosa must endure, waiting for a moment that is far in the distance, while clinging to the things that keep her soul alive. We also see more world-building, as it is revealed how the Citadel, Gas Town, and Bullet Farm interact. It is an immersive experience, different from Fury Road but brilliant in its own way.

Furiosa is a character who has learned to fly under the radar, blend into any setting, and survive at all costs. This means a character who is very careful with her words, resulting in a leading role with around 30 lines of dialogue. You might think this would be a hindrance for a star, but Taylor-Joy makes it a strength. Using everything except words, she conveys a character being molded into her environment, learning to be relentless and endure anything in the name of getting home. That means a performance that is conveyed by expression, movement, and the smallest of gestures. Great actors overcome limitations to tell stories, and the Golden Globe winner rose to the challenge.

Looking back on the film, the actor said that Miller had a very specific idea of how she should hold her face, meaning she could only use her eyes to convey emotions for large portions of the movie. “As an actor, where your job is to be able to telegraph how you’re feeling with absolutely everything at your disposal," she told GQ. "To suddenly have everything gone other than [your eyes] was quite petrifying.”

The gamble paid off, however, as she delivered a soulful, determined performance in the middle of a large-scale action thriller. There are few actors with the discipline, presence, and talent to not only take up the mantle of a beloved character, but to make it their own, and that’s exactly what Taylor-Joy does. For all the many hits of her past, and the exciting projects to come, Furiosa: A Mad Max Saga has a special place in her filmography.

46 Years Later, the Most Influential Thriller of All Time Is Officially Free on YouTube

📅 Nov 22, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

There’s no better time to return to Camp Blood

There’s no better time to return to Camp Blood

Over the years, corporations and filmmakers alike have eagerly sought to revisit the origins of horror classics (e.g., Bates Motel and Psycho, Welcome to Derry and IT, etc.) for modern audiences. It’s an easy way to find successwhen you already have an audience that loves the IP you’re reimagining

Not even the Friday the 13th franchise has escaped the trend of revisiting the past. Crystal Lake, a new series from A24 and Peacock, follows Pamela Voorhees (Betsy Palmer), Jason Voorhees’s deranged mother​​​​​​​. It’s a prequel that, frankly, nobody really asked for (though we kind of liked it anyway), since Jason’s origins have already been explored: Sean S. Cunningham’s 1980s horror film Friday the 13th — available to watch now for free on YouTube.

Following the release of the genre-defining slasher Halloween, Cunningham and writer Victor Miller faced a real challenge: how to stand out in a subgenre flooded with horror movies (Black Christmas, Texas Chainsaw Massacre, to name just a few) trying to ride the slasher wave. Friday the 13th had to be memorable, interesting, and stand out.

On the surface, Friday the 13th doesn’t feel too different. It’s full of horny teens, heavy suspense, and a sense of isolation as a deranged killer hunts and kills them off, one by one. However, unlike other slashers like Halloween, Cunningham takes the brutality one step further by asking two very different but meaningful questions. Careful now, spoilers for a 46-year-old movie ahead.

First, what if the killer were not a man in a mask but a vengeful mother driven mad by society’s disregard for her disabled son? Second, what if Cunningham and Paramount hired Tom Savini, famous for his work on George A. Romero’s Dawn of the Dead, to create some of the most gruesome deaths audiences had yet seen on the silver screen?

The result is one of the bloodiest slashers of all time, one that shook up perceptions of how grotesque the horror subgenre could be and who, exactly, could be the one dealing out those kills in the first place.

Slashers like Halloween and Black Christmas have some gnarly kills—who could forget Michael Myers pinning Bob to a pantry door with a large knife or Barb being stabbed with a unicorn figurine’s horn? Savini, however, elevated Friday the 13th’s kills into a bloody art form with shocking, realistic gore.

Savini did more than deliver, too. Just as he used practical effects for Dawn of the Dead, he did so again for several of the film’s kills. One especially memorable kill is counselor Jack’s, in which he’s stabbed in the neck as he lies in bed. Using a prosthetic neck piece and a pump, fake blood was pumped to create a blood-spurt effect that, for its time, looked remarkably realistic.

The most significant thing that set Friday the 13th apart from its predecessors, however, was its killer: Pamela Voorhees. The slasher genre was no longer defined by masculine rage, but by a mother’s love. Even in the modern era of horror, slashers still feature far more male than female killers, but in 1980, Mrs. Voorhees was undoubtedly one of a kind.

Furthermore, for as deranged and murderous as she was, she exerted a strange, empathetic pull on audiences. Yes, murder is wrong — but you can’t deny that she loved her son. Unlike Michael Myers, who is often depicted as evil incarnate, Mrs. Voorhees has a complexity that made her brutal motives more compelling.

46 years later, Mrs. Voorhees's story begins anew with Crystal Lake. It’s unclear whether the series will get a second season, but even if it doesn’t, fans of Jason and his diabolical family tree needn’t worry. You can never go wrong with the original.

Friday the 13th is available to stream now for free on YouTube.

Quentin Tarantino's Western Thriller Masterpiece Is Officially Free on Streaming

📅 Nov 21, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Brian VanHooker is a Pop Culture Writer specializing in Celebrity Interviews and In-Depth Oral Histories in the areas of Comedy, TV, Film, Cartoons, Comic Books, 80s/90s Nostalgia, Fatherhood, Retro Games and Food (especially Pizza). He was the Senior Features Writer at Cracked and a Staff Writer at MEL. He has written for Inverse, Inside Hook, Maxim, Men's Health, Fatherly, SFGate and more. He's the co-creator of the comic book/cartoon Barnum & Elwood and The Tramp, an award-winning comedy pilot starring John O’Hurley. He also hosts a TMNT interview podcast called Turtle Tracks and was once called a “Good Guy” by Mr. T.

As far as Quentin Tarantino movies go, just about everyone loves Pulp Fiction. Reservoir Dogs and Once Upon a Time… in Hollywood are also widely regarded, while the Kill Bill films and Django Unchained each have a pretty fervent fanbase. But with so many great entries in his filmography, it’s inevitable that at least a few of his movies would get lost in the shuffle. One of those often overlooked films is his 2015 Western thriller, which may not be top-tier Tarantino, but it’s still an excellent film that combines the strengths of two of his previous entries.

The Hateful Eight, which is currently free to stream (with ads) on Tubi, takes place in the 1870s in the wintry Wyoming Territory. Kurt Russell is John "the Hangman" Ruth, a bounty hunter bringing in Daisy Domergue (Jennifer Jason Leigh), a gang member with a big price on her head. He’ll collect his bounty in the town of Red Rock, with a stopover at a place called Minnie's Haberdashery on the way, but Ruth’s driver (James Parks) has to stay ahead of an impending blizzard.

On the way to Minnie’s, Ruth’s carriage picks up two unexpected passengers, both of whom lost their horses in the snow and will surely die in the blizzard. First there’s Major Marquis Warren (Samuel L. Jackson), a fellow bounty hunter and former Union Army general​​​​​​​ who keeps a personal letter to him from Abraham Lincoln in his breast pocket. The second unexpected passenger is Chris Mannix (Walton Goggins), the new sheriff of Red Rock and the son of a Confederate war hero. While Ruth is suspicious of both of them, he decides to let them join him on the way to Minnie’s.

Once they arrive at Minnie’s, however, Minnie isn’t there. Instead, the haberdashery has apparently been left​​​​​​​ in the care of a Mexican man named Señor Bob (Demián Bichir) while Minnie is visiting her mother. Also present are three other men​​​​​​​ that Warren, a frequent guest of Minnie’s, doesn’t recognize. As the entire group is snowed in for the night, it’s eventually revealed​​​​​​​ that nobody is quite who they say they are and bloodshed ultimately ensues.

Like I said, The Hateful Eight is not top-tier Tarantino. Tarantino’s best work has generally come when he’s charting new territory for himself. Inglourious Basterds​​​​​​​ was his first war movie, and with its heist-like plot and unabashed joy in rewriting history, it showed what Tarantino could do with that genre. Django Unchained was his first Western, and, with its heavy influence from Spaghetti Westerns and Tarantino’s trademark talkative, over-the-top characters and depiction of extreme violence, it showed what a Tarantino Western looks like.

The Hateful Eight doesn’t do that. In fact, it can pretty easily be summed up in classic Hollywood this-meets-that fashion: The Hateful Eight is Django Unchained meets Reservoir Dogs.

It being another Western with broad, loud, talkative characters, it has the same feel as Django Unchained. Originally, The Hateful Eight was a Django sequel called Django in White Hell, but Tarantino found that Django didn’t fit the story. And while Django isn't in it, it’s clear that this movie is in the same ballpark.

As for the Reservoir Dogs part of it, it is narratively similar, with it being tightly contained primarily in one location, with a bunch of untrustworthy characters who don’t really know each other very well. It’s also a bit of a whodunit, just like Reservoir Dogs is with its “Who’s the rat?” plot thread.

None of this is to say that The Hateful Eight isn't a good movie — not at all. The Hateful Eight is an expertly crafted film which begins with a slow burn and delivers surprise after surprise once the bullets start flying. The movie blends a lot of genres, with elements of a Western, a thriller, and even a bit of broad comedy, but it's most effective as a mystery. Not only does the plot slowly unravel as Ruth tries to figure out who he can trust, but each of the characters does, too​​​​​​​.

Speaking of those characters, there are no good guys in the movie, but there are a lot of bad guys you end up rooting for because they are incredibly entertaining to watch. Samuel L. Jackson’s character is joyously funny, and Kurt Russell’s hangman is a mean grouch with a strange but consistent set of principles. Also, Walton Goggins, who was previously in Django, racks up another memorable Tarantino character in this movie. (Let’s hope he shows up in Tarantino’s 10th flick and takes a break from those damn Walmart commercials already).

While it stays firmly within previously charted territory for Tarantino, The Hateful Eight is very entertaining and very funny. It has all the hallmarks of a great Tarantino movie, with lots of fun characters, over-the-top violence, and unexpected plot twists. It may not be top-tier Tarantino, but it’s a really good time.

Brandon Sanderson Officially Confirms Major ‘Stormlight Archive’ Update

📅 Nov 21, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Hannah covers horror, sci-fi, and games at Collider, where she writes news and features, reviews new releases, and interviews the people who make them. She’s helping build out Collider’s gaming coverage, with Pokémon as one of her specialties. If she isn’t writing about a monster, she’s probably watching one on a tape from her VHS collection.

The last time Brandon Sanderson took readers to Roshar for a full-length Stormlight Archive novel, he gave them an ending and a fairly long wait. Wind and Truth closed out the first five-book arc in December 2024. When the story picks up again, roughly a decade will have passed for the people who live on Roshar. The question for readers has been how many of those years they’ll have to sit through themselves.

Sanderson originally had quite a bit on his desk between the two halves of The Stormlight Archive. In his 2024 year-end update, he put an entire Mistborn trilogy and two sequels to Elantris ahead of his return to the series. He’d also set aside time for Horneater, a novella that would follow Rock during the events of Rhythm of War. Two years later, the schedule has changed enough that Sanderson is making room for Roshar while he’s still working on Mistborn.

Sanderson has begun outlining The Stormlight Archive Book 6 and plans to start writing the first draft in January 2028. In his November 3 video update, he explained that he’ll work on the outline periodically throughout 2027. That planning extends all the way through Book 10. January 2028 is the date he’s set himself to begin the manuscript. His earlier estimate put publication in 2031, and he has yet to announce a firm release date.

Four worlds and four completely different ideas of what a good life looks like. This is where you belong, what it would ask of you, and what you would get back in return.

You belong somewhere old enough that the mountains have names and somebody can still tell you who gave them. Middle-earth is for people who think the right thing is worth doing even when it will not be finished in their lifetime.

You want the sea, a crew, and absolutely nobody telling you where you are allowed to go. The Grand Line is the world for people whose main ambition in life is finding out what is over there.

You would rather be feared and useful than liked and useless. The Continent is a world where the monsters are the straightforward part of the job and the people are where it all gets complicated.

You want the myths to be real and loose in the actual world, and you want to find out at about thirteen that you were never anywhere near as ordinary as everybody kept telling you.

“My big write for next year is to try to finish Ghostbloods 2 and 3,” Sanderson said in the November update. Ghostbloods is the next Mistborn trilogy, and Sanderson made an unusual publishing decision for those three books: he wants to finish writing all of them before releasing the first. That gives readers following both series two different calendars to keep straight, because Sanderson could be drafting Geralt-free adventures on Roshar while fans are still waiting to read the Mistborn books he wrote before them.

The Elantris sequels have a less certain spot on that calendar. Sanderson previously hoped to write them between Ghostbloods installments, but in his 2025 year-end update he cited his growing Hollywood workload as a reason to leave himself more time, work that now includes the screenplay for the Mistborn movie. For readers looking for the next concrete update on Book 6, Sanderson said he intends to add it to the progress tracker on his website. The first milestone fans will be able to follow there is the outline for the return to Roshar.

Do You Always Have to Sacrifice Passion for Emotional Intimacy?

📅 Nov 21, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Do you remember the beginning of your relationship with your partner? When you would be turned on by just the thought of your fingers sharing space with theirs? When you’d bust into the apartment movie-style, tossing aside layers of clothes because you just couldn’t wait to get to skin?

Now, your relationship looks a little different. There are no mental gymnastics before grabbing your partner’s hand and you don’t mind putting your clothes away nicely before jumping into bed. It’s a comfortable, cozy phase—one that you worked hard and long to get to.

But sometimes, you might wish you could have both.

We’ve collectively accepted that a long-term relationship somehow means the end of passion. You spend months or even years learning everything you can about your partner. You’ve gained trust, a strong foundation, and yet, the passion seems to have disappeared with the uncertainty.

“The comfort you worked so hard to build may be what’s subtly killing your desire for your partner,” certified sex therapist Dr. Mindy DeSeta, PhD tells Cosmopolitan.

She explains that over time, we unknowingly become complacent about romancing our partner. All of the exciting unknowns of the beginning of a relationship (Do they want to kiss you? Do they want to come home with you?) eventually fade away.

“You may be intimately familiar with your partner's moves after a number of years, and that's a beautiful thing,” Dr. DeSeta says. “But repetition can dull the excitement of physical intimacy.”

But contrary to what we’ve all learned to accept, this isn’t an inevitable diagnosis for your long-term relationship. It is possible to have both emotional intimacy and sexual passion. You just have to work for it.

“Your sex life will suffer if you do not actively continue to learn your partner’s desires,” Dr. DeSeta says.

You might think that you know everything there is to know already about your partner. But as we get older, our desires change—even if our partner doesn’t.

“Your pleasure is constantly evolving and if you want to continue to have a thriving and exciting sex life, a couple must be able to talk about most recent desires,” she says. “What turned you on at 25 may not still turn you on at 40.”

This is why communicating your desires and feelings to your partner is so important. Sure, you and your partner may be pros at working through disagreements and emotionally supporting each other through life’s changes—but does that energy translate into your sex life? Do you share your curiosities? Do you hold space for them telling you they’d like to try something different? If you don’t, it might be time to make a change.

First, in order to feel sexy with your partner, you need to feel sexy yourself. Do something every day that makes you feel sexy and confident,” Dr. DeSeta says. “The foundation of a great sex life starts with each person having a thriving libido.”

Then try to initiate sexy conversation with your partner. Dr. DeSeta suggests planning something that feels tantalizing without the expectation of physical intimacy to follow. Reach each other erotica, talk about new fantasies you’ve had, discuss a trendy sex topic in media.

“Many long-term couples need a prompt to get the sexy conversations started,” she says. They also need reminders to touch each other more.

“Long term couples are notorious for forgetting physical affection,” she says. Dr. DeSeta emphasizes small touches every day. This can look like caressing their back as you pass them in the kitchen or holding hands while you watch your favorite show on the couch. Instead of maneuvering around each other, lean into the touch. It signals to your partner that you see them and want them throughout the day. And just the feeling of being wanted can ignite some of that passion from the early days of your relationship.

Most importantly, you should date your partner every day. Dr. DeSeta recommends “Daily Dates” to couples she sees in therapy. This is when you set aside 20 minutes everyday to make your partner a priority.

“All too often long-term couples put their relationship second, so this tune up is always needed,” she says. “During these 20 minutes the phones are silenced, logistical conversations are off the table, and the time is dedicated to emotionally connecting with their partner.”

By doing this, she explains that you slow down the daily hustle and really learn how to reconnect with your partner. You might even learn a few new things about them in the process.

“If we do not make the intentional effort to keep the passion alive, then you are at risk of falling into autopilot,” Dr.Deseta says. We often think passion and emotional intimacy are at odds with each other, but that’s because we think it’s acceptable to simply fall into our relationship without making an active effort to keep our relationship exciting. And no, you don’t have to be constantly ripping each other’s clothes off or feel nervous in order to have a passionate relationship. But you should aim to feel the same deep desire and attraction to your partner that you did when you first started off—if not more.

‘Your Daughter’ Director on Bonds With Mom, Marianne Khoury, and ‘Uncle Joe,’ Youssef Chahine: ‘Filmmaking Is Really About Healing’

📅 Nov 21, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

For Egyptian filmmaker Sara Shazli, the camera became a way of asking her mother questions she had carried since childhood. Returning to Cairo from film school in Cuba, she would wait in the family kitchen, camera in hand, hoping to understand the woman whose devotion to cinema had so often left her feeling alone.

“Was the camera protecting me from the answers she could give me? Was the camera a way of having her attention?,” Shazli wonders. With cinema occupying such a central place in her mother’s life, filming their conversations offered a way of reaching her through the very medium that had so often kept them apart. Related Stories Alex Gibney-Produced ‘Mohabat,’ Directed by James Longley, Wins at Ji.hlava: The Film Is 'Meticulously Researched and Exquisitely Crafted' Sean Penn, Ava DuVernay and Laura Poitras Turn Out For San Francisco’s Doc Stories

Those encounters became the foundation of “Your Daughter,” Shazli’s documentary about her relationship with her mother Marianne Khoury, the Egyptian producer and filmmaker who spent decades working alongside her uncle, renowned director Youssef Chahine. Premiering at the 47th Cairo Intl. Film Festival, the film charts a daughter’s changing understanding of her mother, from resentment over her childhood absence to an admiration she had never anticipated.

Shazli initially conceived the project as a search for Woody, the nanny who had helped raise her, hoping to reconnect with a figure who had played such an important role in her childhood. But as she developed the film and attended workshops with other filmmakers, their observations helped her recognize a more deeply personal story taking shape in the footage of her mother.

“Someone told me, ‘Look at how you film your mother. You film her with so much love,’” she recalls. “It was actually painful,” she adds, because acknowledging that love meant confronting difficult questions, ones she had avoided for far too long.

Those questions extended beyond Khoury’s absence to the expectations she passed on to her daughter. “I grew up with a mother telling me that you have to be independent from any man in the world,” Shazli says. “But it was so extreme. Why can’t both the dad and the mom help? Why can’t both be responsible?”

Khoury had built her career while shouldering much of the financial burden and everyday demands of family life. Shazli admired her mother’s determination but questioned the sacrifices that came along with it. She sought a different kind of partnership, with a husband who shares childcare and financial responsibilities, rather than carrying those burdens alone.

Her family’s cinematic legacy brought pressures of its own. Growing up around Chahine, whom she affectionately calls “Uncle Joe,” Shazli struggled to establish her own identity as a filmmaker.

“Nobody put that pressure on me,” she says. “It was me, coming from that Chahine family, being compared to such a titan. What are you going to do with all this?” Studying film in Cuba offered distance from her family and from Egypt, helping her discover what she wanted to say as a filmmaker.

“I started to see her differently and I started to feel for her,” Shazli tells Variety of her conversations with Khoury. She came to understand how her mother’s own upbringing had shaped the way she expressed affection. “It did hurt me to realize that she had grown up with a different kind of love, one where affection wasn’t always expressed through time and attention. She gave what she knew. You give what you receive.”

Her growing empathy did not erase the hurt of her childhood, but it allowed her to see Khoury beyond the role of mother. Khoury, who took over Chahine’s production outfit Misr International after his passing, remains as devoted to her work as ever, but Shazli no longer sees that devotion solely through the lens of what she missed growing up.

Becoming a mother herself brought another shift in perspective. “Now I feel like I really admire her,” Shazli says. “How on earth did she manage to do all this?”

“That says a lot about her energy, about how much ambition she has,” she adds. “I’m nowhere near that, and I’m alright with that too. I’m actually happy that I don’t have that because you suffer when you have that. You want to be everywhere and do everything.” But that admiration does not mean she intends to follow the same path.

For Shazli, whose earlier documentary “Back Home” explored her relationship with her father, filmmaking is also a means of working through deeply personal questions. “For me, filmmaking is really about healing,” she says. “If there’s no problem, there’s no film. If there’s nothing to discover, to heal, to dig deep into, there’s no film.”

The Cairo premiere has brought an unexpected response, including from men who recognized their own family relationships in the film. “When somebody comes and tells you, ‘You made me want to speak to my mother,’ or ‘You made me want to speak to my father,’ that’s exactly what it should do,” she says.

But having explored both parents on screen, Shazli is ready to turn toward fiction. “I did a film about my dad. I did a film about my mom. I’m done,” she says. “Now I want to have more fun. I want to write a fiction. I want to work with actors. I want to create differently now.”

Egyptian Music Star Mohamed Mounir Gets Biopic – Doha Industry Days

📅 Nov 21, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

The life and legacy of legendary Egyptian artist Mohamed Mounir is set to be explored in a biopic spearheaded by The Film Committee at Media City Qatar.

The Film Committee has acquired the rights to document Mounir’s life story, with partners on the project set to include a leading regional studio and other partners, which have yet to be unveiled.

The project was announced at the Film Committee’s Doha Industry Days, just hours after the Qatari body gave an update on previously announced biopic Caesar, about Syrian whistleblower Farid Al-Madhhan, announcing Taim Hasan as it star. Related Stories News 'Al Hayba' Star Taim Hasan To Play Syrian Whistleblower Farid Al-Madhhan In Neon-Backed 'Caesar' Biopic News Steven Paul Talks Up Federal Film & TV Tax Credit: "It's Going To Be Good For The World" - Industry Days Doha

Mounir’s career spans nearly five decades across music, film and television. Watch on Deadline

His 1981 breakthrough album ‘Shababeek’ established him as one of Egypt’s most distinctive artists, with his work characterized by its fusion of Nubian folk and Arabic pop with Western genres like jazz, blues, and reggae.

He is known to his fans across the Middle East and beyond as “El King” in reference to his 1998 album and play ‘El Malek Howwa El Malek’ ((The King is The King).

Also working in cinema, his some 20 credits including collaborations with late Egyptian filmmaker Youssef Chahine on films including Egyptian Story (1982) and Destiny, which played in Out of Competition in Cannes in 1997 and was then selected as Egypt’s Oscar entry.

Mounir was born into a Nubian family in a village in Southern Egypt, where he spent his early childhood. His family moved to Cairo, after the village was resettled due to the construction of the Aswan Dam in the 1960s, where his singing voice was discovered when he sang at a family gathering.

The Film Committee said the biopic will trace Mounir’s journey from his humble beginnings to becoming one of the Arab world’s most celebrated and influential artists, revealing untold details, surprises, stories and anecdotes.

“Mohamed Mounir’s music holds a special place in the lives of audiences across Egypt and the Arab world,” said Film Committee at Media City Qatar Chair Hassan Al Thawadi.

“Bringing his story to the big screen is an opportunity to celebrate his artistry, the cultural heritage his work represents and its lasting social relevance, a story that connects generations of fans across the region and has the power to resonate with audiences around the world,” he continued.

“This project also builds on our recent investment in extraordinary life stories from the region and reflects our ambition to develop a world-leading slate of biographical films that brings these stories to audiences globally.”

Alongside the feature film, plans include a behind-the-scenes documentary featuring interviews with Mounir and those who have lived and worked alongside him, as well as documenting the development and production of the biopic.

With development in the early stages, the hunt is now on for an actor to play Mounir.

These 8 Single-Season Fantasy Shows Are Masterpieces

📅 Nov 21, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Fantasy TV is greatly known for thriving on sprawling worlds and long-running sagas, multi-season chaos that offers thrilling character journeys. But sometimes the genre produces rare and fantastic hits that deliver just a single season. That one-season hit often wields an unforgettable story with ambitious themes, striking visual direction, and an emotionally satisfying arc—even when it concludes on a slight cliffhanger.

Masterful series like the layered superhero drama, Watchmen, that produces an alternate version of history, and the cult classic gem, Neverwhere, that explores a hidden magical London, are just two series in the genre that demonstrate just how brilliant a one-season fantasy icon can truly be. Compiled on this list are the TV shows that wield the title of masterpiece, despite their single-season status.

Over the Garden Wall is an animated show that may wield a short runtime, but the series' fairytale-like storytelling and haunting visuals ensured its lasting legacy as a masterful fantasy masterpiece. The Cartoon Network series follows a pair of lost brothers, Wirt (Elijah Wood) and Greg (Collin Dean), who stumble into "the Unknown," where they meet several unusual characters on their strange journey home.

Over the Garden Wall somehow captures wonder and melancholy together perfectly. It's an animated series that’s beautifully strange and skillfully atmospheric. Over the Garden Wall is a truly unique ride that is presented like a traditional storybook tale, but with an eerie atmosphere, darker tone, and escalating moral stakes. What makes it a masterful one-season watch is that every episode acts like a "chapter" that still advances the character and theme: adolescence vs. childhood, fear vs. hope, and the seduction of giving up when the going gets tough. Over the Garden Wall is definitely a fantastic fantasy great that stands as a tightly crafted masterpiece despite its status as a single-season series.

This brilliant fantasy series is one of the finest of the genre that has ever been brought to screens, and unfortunately, only has one season to its name. My Lady Jane is an alternate-history fantasy, focusing on the bold and ambitious Lady Jane Grey (Emily Bader), who is forced into marriage and involuntarily thrust into lethal court politics.

My Lady Jane is a reimagining of English history with a playful fantasy twist. The show's premise is genuinely intriguing as it delivers magical lore and thrilling political drama. My Lady Jane's energetic storytelling and clever take on the past make it an extremely entertaining single-season masterpiece. Without withholding its payoff to later seasons, the show commits to a full season-long story arc, including political affairs, romance, and a heady tone. It is truly unfortunate that My Lady Jane never got the chance to expand its story due to its cancellation, but it does make for a great one-season fantasy gem.

Watchmen is an expansion of the iconic comic universe that transports audiences into a daring alternate-history fantasy drama. Set in a world where masked vigilantes are treated as outlaws, the series focuses on detective Angela Abar aka Sister Night (Regina King), as she investigates conspiracies that reawaken old ideological violence.

Watchmen is a quality limited series that honestly outdoes those with a much longer run time. The show's bold themes, stunning performances, and meticulous writing prove that with just one season, a story of excellent caliber can be brought to audiences. Watchmen stands as a powerful fantasy jewel that easily earned itself a strong reputation as a television masterpiece. While using genre spectacles like superheroes, dystopia, and mystery to do precise thematic work, the show builds on the usual comic-book tropes and turns them into a sharp social commentary. Watchmen is often lauded as one of the best miniseries to be produced by HBO, solidifying it as the perfect one-season epic for this list of masterpieces.

This Marvel TV series is a skillful blend of classic television nostalgia and epic superhero fantasy. WandaVision initially begins with superheroes, Wanda Maximoff (Elizabeth Olsen) and Vision (Paul Bettany), as they live a happy, sitcom-shaped suburban life as a married couple. It isn't very long before their world begins to fracture, as the two start to notice strange happenings that seem to have a single source—Wanda.

WandaVision is initially an incredibly quirky series that honestly stumps most audiences who are watching it for the first time. But it doesn't take very long to see why most viewers are quick to praise the iconic series. WandaVision hosts inventive storytelling and fantastic emotional depth that elevate its one-season arc into a fully realized fantasy masterpiece that's truly able to captivate audiences. Its story may be short, but WandaVision ranks high amongst some of the absolute best in the fantasy genre.

Four worlds and four completely different ideas of what a good life looks like. This is where you belong, what it would ask of you, and what you would get back in return.

You belong somewhere old enough that the mountains have names and somebody can still tell you who gave them. Middle-earth is for people who think the right thing is worth doing even when it will not be finished in their lifetime.

You want the sea, a crew, and absolutely nobody telling you where you are allowed to go. The Grand Line is the world for people whose main ambition in life is finding out what is over there.

You would rather be feared and useful than liked and useless. The Continent is a world where the monsters are the straightforward part of the job and the people are where it all gets complicated.

You want the myths to be real and loose in the actual world, and you want to find out at about thirteen that you were never anywhere near as ordinary as everybody kept telling you.

Lovecraft Country is an intriguingly fearless mesh of horror, fantasy, and historical commentary. Set in 1950s America, the story centers on Atticus Freeman (Jonathan Majors) as he, along with Letitia "Leti" Lewis (Jurnee Smollett) and Hippolyta Freeman (Aunjanue Ellis-Taylor), travels across the country in search of his missing father, only to encounter not only racist terror, but also supernatural monsters.

Lovecraft Country is a rather bold series with a very entertaining concept; unfortunately, it never made it far, as it was cancelled rather quickly. Despite that, the show is good enough as a one-season gem that delivers an incredible story. With a combination of American history and cosmic horror, Lovecraft Country offers a unique narrative that is significantly memorable despite its status as a single-season series. From production design to music choices, Lovecraft's cultural work only makes its story feel all the more historical and mythic, marking it as the perfect addition to this list of masterful one-season watches.

This unfortunately cancelled series came and left in a burst of creativity and thrill. The Bastard Son & The Devil Himself is a book-to-screen coming-of-age adaptation that follows the young Nathan (Jay Lycurgo), who finds himself caught in between warring witch clans as the son of a feared killer.

The Bastard Son & The Devil Himself is extremely addictive, but horrendously underrated. It's a one-season masterpiece for its intriguing story and fantastic performances. The Bastard Son & The Devil Himself is genuinely entertaining, and its abrupt cancellation definitely disappointed many of its fans. Even with the dark fantasy's brief run, The Bastard Son & The Devil Himself, with its gripping storytelling, has earned its place among some of fantasy's most overlooked masterpieces, making it the ideal pick for this list of peak gems in the vast genre.

Neverwhere is based on Neil Gaiman's novel of the same name and stands as one of fantasy’s greatest cult favorites. The beloved series centers around Richard Mayhew (Gary Bakewell) as he helps an injured girl named Door (Laura Fraser), only to find himself erased from ordinary London and pulled into the hidden fantasy underworld known as "London Below."

With an imaginative premise and a thrillingly eerie atmosphere, Neverwhere is a stunning fantasy masterpiece that delivers a mysterious underground realm filled with hidden magic and strange characters. The show's tightly contained narrative and inventive world never feel like it's merely a one-season story; instead, Neverwhere offers audiences a fully realized narrative. The 1996 series further showcases its masterpiece status with vivid worldbuilding, clear stakes, and a complete transformation of its protagonist. Neverwhere does wield that BBC flair, but the series is only enhanced by it, giving it a dream-like atmosphere. The series concludes with a resolution that feels like a quality payoff, marking it as a bingeable watch that delivers a complete fantasy masterpiece that’s worth diving into.

This iconic horror series may not be in the realm of traditional fantasy, but the supernatural gem is a ghostly fantasy drama that definitely counts as a memorable single-season masterpiece. The Haunting of Hill House follows the fractured Crain family, who are forced to relive the trauma of their haunted childhood home as the story switches between the past and present, showcasing how the family's adult lives are shaped by what they survived inside "Hill House."

The Haunting of Hill House wields a chilling blend of supernatural horror and family drama, masterfully delivering a series rife with great scares and surprising amounts of emotional depth. The show is a limited series, so it feels like it's built perfectly for a single season. Each episode is both a puzzle piece and a character study—a genuine full thesis statement. With fantastic direction and incredible production design, the overall craftsmanship of The Haunting of Hill House only proves that a single season show can still offer audiences quality entertainment.

GTA 6 Launch Stats Are In: Over 50% Of Active Console Users Played

📅 Nov 21, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

GTA 6 has already outgrossed the entire box office run of Avengers: Endgame, the highest-grossing movie in history — and the sheer scale of that success is represented in the launch day stats.

According to Mat Piscatella, senior director and video game industry advisor at Circana, over 50% of all active consoler users played GTA 6 at launch, averaging 494–513 minutes of playtime, or roughly 8 hours.

That does not mean 50% of all console users played it.

"For players active on November 19th, 61% of US PlayStation 5 and 56% of global Xbox players engaged with Grand Theft Auto 6," he posted to Bluesky. "PS5 GTA 6 players averaged 513 minutes that day, Xbox players 494. Both the DAU and minutes played engagement rates are absolutely bonkers."

Piscatella doesn't have the exact stats to hand, explaining that it's "not exactly easy to go back and sort daily active engagement rates," but to his recollection, during his time at Circana, there has been "nothing near this."

The only game second to GTA 6 on launch was Fortnite, accounting for 10% of all active players across both platforms. Clearly, the Pirate Shop Access tokens are working.

As for what GTA 6 players were up to before the game launched, Piscatella had some additional stats, sharing the top-five games between November 6 and 19:

$3.7 billion in revenue within 24 hours, "the biggest entertainment launch of all time," accounting for over 50% of all active console users — those numbers are nothing to scoff at.

In fact, in less than a week, with 32.5 million copies sold, GTA 6 is already one of the best-selling games of all-time. It currently sits on the edge of the top 30 after overtaking Super Mario Odyssey, Borderlands 2, and Monster Hunter: World.

A lot of people felt compelled to buy something they could play GTA 6 on after being shown a lot more of it.

Considering that GTA 5 is the third best-selling game of all-time, behind only Minecraft and Tetris (clearly, the secret to selling hundreds of millions of copies is blocks), it's easy to imagine GTA 6 landing somewhere near the top.

Almost six years after the PS5 launched, almost two million people are still playing GTA on PS4.

Marion Cotillard Says ‘I’m Not Here to Judge Movies’ as Marrakech Jury President and ‘Doesn’t Want Any Information’ Before Watching: ‘Like a Virgin’

📅 Nov 21, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Marion Cotillard, who serves as the jury president at this year’s Marrakech Film Festival, says she prefers watching films with “as little information as possible.”

“I don’t know anything about the movies we’re gonna see, I haven’t seen a single image,” the Oscar-winning French actress said at the festival’s jury press conference on Saturday morning. “That’s how I love to see movies, I don’t want to have any information.” Related Stories Laura Poitras Turns Lens on ICE's 'Paramilitary Occupation' of Minneapolis in 'They’re Here' Ji.hlava New Visions Award Winner Karim Akalay Mixes Magic With ‘Anti-Social Realism’ in ‘6TM’: ‘It’s About Collective Strength Rather Than One Hero’

“I want to be like a virgin,” she quipped.

Ironically, she said she doesn’t think her role on the jury is to “judge movies.” “I consider myself as part of an audience: I’m here to be touched, to be taken by stories.”

Cotillard was also asked about “Inception,” in which she starred alongside Leonardo DiCaprio. Does she understand Christopher Nolan’s famously complex film? “I don’t know…I thought I had,” she said to laughter. “Maybe I have. And maybe I haven’t.”

The nine-member jury includes “Sex Education” and “Barbie” star Emma Mackey; “Phantom Thread” standout Vicky Krieps; Danish actor Claes Bang, who featured in Ruben Östlund’s Palme d’Or winner “The Square”; French actor-turned-director Gilles Lellouche, known for “Beating Hearts”; “The Voice of Hind Rajab” helmer Kaouther Ben Hania; “All of Us Strangers” filmmaker Andrew Haigh; Franco-Senegalese helmer Ramata-Toulaye Sy; and Moroccan director-producer Saïd Hamich Benlarbi.

The jury will award the festival’s Étoile d’Or to one of the 14 films in competition, which consists of first and second features.

Cotillard said her only expectation she has in helping select this year’s winner is “to be shaken, to be moved.”

“When I can watch a movie and I can laugh and smile and be questioned at the same time,” she continued. “When there’s a story that on the paper I wouldn’t be very interested [in] and the filmmaker who tells the story will open a new vision for me.”

She summed it up simply: “How do you judge art anyway?”

Mackey agrees with Cotillard that her role on the jury is to “receive the films as a spectator. “We’re here to learn, its very pure somehow,” the “Sex Education” star said. “I don’t pretend that I know everything and I’m here to learn and to absorb these films as as a cinema lover.”

“It’s an honor and I’m happy to spend a week watching two films a day, that’s amazing,” she added.

Jury members were also asked about the relatively strong female representation at Marrakech and its broader implications for gender equality in the industry. This year, women hold a majority of seats on the nine-member jury while six of the 14 films in competition were directed by women.

Krieps pointed to her role as an Irish maid in Ryan Murphy’s “Monster: The Lizzie Borden Story” to illustrate the importance of female representation on screen. “I think women have had the role of oppressed and silent,” she said. “Often we had to hide, we had to disappear because otherwise we weren’t safe.”

“I think the role I play in ‘Monster’ is someone who becomes very visible in a time when people were supposed to be invisible,” she continued, adding that her character’s wild red hair and contemporary costuming could “help people see differently in the future.”

Sy, meanwhile, offered a more succinct response, suggesting that true progress will come when female representation is no longer a point of discussion.

“The day when we stop counting how many women there are in juries, in jobs, as directors, then it will be normal,” the Sengalese filmmaker said to enthusiastic applause. “We have to stop asking the question and counting how many there are.”

On Friday night, the festival opened with Jesse Eisenberg’s “The Debut,” a musical comedy starring Julianne Moore as a shy New Jersey housewife who auditions for a regional production. Though Eisenberg and Moore weren’t in attendance, the opening ceremony was still a starry affair with guests including Tilda Swinton and “Knives Out” director Rian Johnson, who is giving a talk at the festival on Sunday.

Mackey is next playing the White Witch in Greta Gerwig’s “Narnia: The Magician’s Nephew,” and told Variety on the red carpet that she was excited to meet Swinton — who played the role in the original film trilogy — for the first time at Marrakech.

“It’s thrilling, my absolute dream person,” she said, adding: “She’s the queen. I wouldn’t even know how to breathe around her, I don’t think.”

The Marrakech Film Festival runs through Nov. 28.

Indian Auteur Girish Kasaravalli Looks Back on 50 Years of Cinema at IFFI Masterclass: ‘All My Films Talk About Othering’

📅 Nov 21, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Indian auteur Girish Kasaravalli looked back on his career in Kannada-language cinema at the 57th International Film Festival of India (IFFI) in Goa, in a masterclass titled “The Moral Universe of Parallel Cinema: 50 Years of Girish Kasaravalli.”

The 75-year-old spoke about the moral contradictions of society and the relationship between cinematic form and social content. He also argued that even entertainment is a political act. The session was moderated by Variety‘s Asia editor Naman Ramachandran.

Across films such as “Ghatashraddha” (1977), “Tabarana Kathe” (1986), “Thaayi Saheba” (1997), “Dweepa” (2002) and “Gulabi Talkies” (2008), Kasaravalli has kept returning to one question: how societies exclude individuals and communities, and how people who are themselves victims of oppressive structures can also become complicit in perpetuating them.

Asked whether the 1977 Kannada classic “Ghatashraddha,” which examines religious orthodoxy and social exclusion, still spoke to contemporary India, Kasaravalli said: “All my films talk about othering. I always talk about how society looks at some people from a different lens, and in that process they make them ‘other.'” He described this as “the biggest cruelty an individual or a group of people can impart on another person.”

Asked whether he considered himself a political filmmaker, Kasaravalli said: “Whoever makes something, whether he makes mainstream cinema or art cinema, he is making a political statement, either in support of the dominant ideology or opposing the dominant ideology. He cannot avoid it.” There is nothing called only entertainment, he added, and even entertainment is a political form.

On his characters, he said: “I don’t want any of my characters to become a kind of a mouthpiece to a certain ideology.” Drawing on Jean-Luc Godard, he compared understanding a character to buying a house: one must examine it from the outside and then from within before making a judgment. Similarly, a filmmaker must inhabit a character and then step back to assess it. For Kasaravalli, characters are too complex for a filmmaker to easily take sides.

On the evolution of his cinematic language, Kasaravalli described the visual construction of “Ghatashraddha,” as “ritualistic cinema.” “The style is not imposed on it; it comes from within the content itself,” he said. Since “Ghatashraddha” was about a ritual, he felt its cinematic form should reflect that structure.

Kasaravalli said he could not write a script until he had clarity about its images. “For me, the film is not merely making statements; it’s an attempt by the filmmaker to impart an experience.” He described writing detailed instructions for his crew, down to which hand a character uses to lift a glass, because even that changes the composition of a shot.

This attention to the relationship between character and environment shaped “Gulabi Talkies.” Kasaravalli told cinematographer S. Ramachandra that the coastal community had to be as important as the protagonist. He wanted the images to feel like “actuality coverage,” not a predetermined visual construction. Rejecting conventional scenic shots of the sea and rocks, he told his cinematographer: “Film should not have any pretty image.” They chose 16mm, allowing the grain to contribute to the film’s sense of actuality.

The discussion of visual aesthetics brought him to Cuban filmmaker Julio García Espinosa’s idea of “imperfect cinema,” which, for Kasaravalli, challenged the assumption that Western aesthetics should define cinematic perfection. “I don’t want to take your aesthetic sensibility as my sensibility,” he said, quoting Espinosa. Kasaravalli said each country and each region has its own aesthetic concerns. “We should make films according to that condition; we should never imitate the West. Why should I be perfect? I should be loyal to my own milieu.”

Asked whether parallel cinema belonged to a particular period, Kasaravalli disagreed, pointing to notable films being made across the country today. The larger problem, he said, was reaching audiences. Of streaming platforms, he said: “We all thought it is going to be a solution for all of us. But OTT [streaming] is exactly doing what the mainstream distribution network was doing earlier.”

In the audience Q&A, he addressed open endings and whether a filmmaker should provide answers. “A filmmaker who says that he knows answers for all the questions in life is telling a lie,” he said. “He is also as confused as you are. He only throws up options so that you can start thinking. There is nothing called closure… life is always open.”

As the session concluded, IFFI festival director Ashutosh Gowariker described Kasaravalli’s work as “textbooks,” saying he had returned to “Ghatashraddha” while developing a new film. He also singled out the filmmaker’s close-ups. “You let your actors simply be. You make the audience think and anticipate what the character is going through,” Gowariker said.

UFC Qatar weigh-in results: Jiri Prochazka vs. Navajo Stirling official, 1 fighter misses weight

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

The UFC Qatar official weigh-ins were mostly without drama, especially with the main card set, but preliminary fighter Tahir Abdullayev spoiled what could have been a perfect session.

Ahead of Saturday's event at Ali Bin Hamad al-Attiyah Arena in Doha, Qatar, 24 fighters stepped to the scale and Abdullayev was the lone miss Friday morning. Abdullayev weighed in at 172 pounds, one pound over the limit for a non-title welterweight bout, while his opponent Jake Matthews weighed in at 170 pounds.

The UFC later announced that Abdullayev vs. Matthews will proceed at a catchweight, with Abdullayev forfeiting 20 percent of his purse as penalty.

Fortunately for fans, headliners Jiri Prochazka and Navajo Stirling weighed in without a hitch, with both fighters making a statement by registering at the championship limit of 205 pounds. Co-main event featherweights Aljamain Sterling and Kevin Vallejos also hit championship weight at 145 pounds on the dot.

Also of note, two-time featherweight title challenger Brian Ortega successfully made weight as he moves up a division, coming in at 155.5 pounds. His opponent, veteran lightweight Dan Hooker, weighed in at 156 pounds.

‘Hunger Games: Sunrise On The Reaping’ 2nd Best Franchise Previews With $21M+; Scorches ‘Wicked’ & ‘The Odyssey’ – Box Office

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

No Wicked, no Moana, no problem, because Lionsgate is saving the Thanksgiving week box office with The Hunger Games: Sunrise on the Reaping which is posting $21.25M in previews, the second best ever for the franchise behind 2013’s Hunger Games: Catching Fire which lit $25.2M in its previews. Sunrise‘s money is currently the second-best previews YTD after Sony’s Spider-Man: Brand New Day ($72M, $360M) and ahead of Universal’s The Odyssey ($17.6M previews, $123.5M opening).

Other previews that Sunrise on the Reaping is beating include Universal’s Wicked from two years ($19.2M; $112.5M opening); for the longest time we heard that the latest feature take of a Suzanne Collins novel was outstripping the presales of that Ariana Grande and Cynthia Erivo female skewing musical. It’s also ahead of Lionsgate’s Michael ($12.6M previews, $97.2M opening), The Devil Wears Prada 2 ($10M, $76.7M opening) and light years ahead of the previous Hunger Games: Ballad of Songbirds and Snakes ($5.75M previews, $44.6M opening) which had its campaign impacted by the 2023 actors strike, not to mention the Collins novel about the origins of Coriolanus Snow wasn’t a fan-fave. Sunrise on the Reaping was beloved by fans in its early story about the Woody Harrelson mentor character, Haymitch Abernathy. Related Stories News Everything We Know About The 'Hunger Games: Sunrise On The Reaping' Movie So Far News 'Hunger Games: Sunrise On The Reaping' To Shine Brightly Around The World With $160M-$190M WW Debut - Box Office Preview

When it comes to the all-time preview record for a Lionsgate title, that belongs to Twilight Saga: Breaking Dawn – Part 2 which made $30.4M from midnight shows leading to a $141M opening back in mid-November 2012. Watch on Deadline

Now, Sunrise on the Reaping‘s advance money was minted with $5M via early access PLF showtimes on Wednesday, with another $16.25M collected tonight per sources. Catching Fire‘s back in the day began warming up with 10PM Thursday night shows. Note Wicked‘s previews were comprised of Amazon promotional screenings, Wednesday night PLF shows and an $8M Thursday.

With a great preview start, it’s fair to say that a $100M opening is well in store for the net $150M priced Sunrise on the Reaping, especially with a 95% audience score on Rotten Tomatoes, which is the best ever in the franchise ahead of Catching Fire (89%), Ballad of Songbirds & Snakes (89%), Hunger Games (81%), Mockingjay Part 2 (71%), and Mockingjay Part 1 (66%).

Overseas, industry projects have risen from a $70M forecast to $86M opening.

Critics aren’t hot on the fifth Hunger Games movie from director Francis Lawrence (the original 2012 movie was helmed by Gary Ross) with an RT score of 68% fresh. However, even if Katniss was shooting Cupid’s arrows at critics, they’d still play hard to get, for the best reviews in the series were a a while ago with Catching Fire (90% certified fresh) and Hunger Games (85% certified fresh). Ever since the Mockingjays (both at 70%), critics’ love for Hunger Games has waned with Songbirds and Snakes being the worst reviewed in franchise at 64%.

The Hunger Games franchise through its five previous movies has minted $3.3 billion at the global box office.

Lionsgate Motion Picture Chair Adam Fogelson on why Sunrise on the Reaping is bigger than Songbirds & Snakes at the B.O.:

Adam Fogelson, chairman of @Lionsgate's Motion Picture Group, on the box office predictions for #HungerGames 'Sunrise on the Reaping' https://t.co/KlV7dEUVx8 pic.twitter.com/Uyj48lcI6P

Going wide this weekend in 2,477 is Amazon MGM Studio’s I Play Rocky after two weekends of limited play, the pic’s B.O. currently at $1.07M.

Darsheel Safary & Zain Khan Durrani Leading Alok Naik’s ‘Cyber’

EXCLUSIVE: Darsheel Safary and Zain Khan Durrani are leading an Indian drama about cybercrime from director Alok Naik.

Cyber will follow two young unemployed computer engineering graduates whose lives take an unexpected turn when they get an opportunity to work with a cyber agency. What begins as a chance to prove their abilities soon draws them into the complex world of cybercrime and investigations.

While being a story on investigating cybercrimes, producers say it is also a “blend of thrills, friendship and contemporary social concerns.” Related Stories News Bae Is Back: Prime Video Sets Launch Date For 'Call Me Bae' Season 2; Watch Teaser News E. Niwas Returns To Feature Directing With Supernatural Comedy-Adventure 'Kurla Boyz' From Abundantia Entertainment & One Entertainment Films

Eminem Takes a Trip Down Memory Lane for ‘The Slim Shady Show’-Inspired Episode of Spotify’s ‘Billions Club’

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Em gets quizzed about his classic hits by his alter ego.

Eminem joined forces with Spotify to celebrate the Detroit legend becoming part of the streaming giant’s Billions Club with an episode from the popular series, which arrived on Wednesday (Nov. 18).

In typical Em fashion, he put his own creative spin on the visual, drawing inspiration from his animated The Slim Shady Show from 2000.

In the episode, Eminem appears at the Spotify office surrounded by Billions Club accolades, and then he’s fittingly served a dish of his mom’s spaghetti in the “Lose Yourself” plaque.

The “Not Afraid” rapper gets annoyed when sauce spills on his new Air Jordan 4s, and then an old friend taking on his Slim Shady persona greets him from the center of the pasta dish.

Em is then pulled into an animated world and takes a trip down memory lane while getting quizzed on his classic hits that have joined the Billions Club over the years, including “Not Afraid,” “Stan,” “Lose Yourself,” “Love the Way You Lie” with Rihanna, “Godzilla” featuring Juice WRLD, “Mockingbird,” “Superman,” “Till I Collapse,” “Rap God” and Akon’s “Smack That.”

The episode closes with Em returning to the real world and threatening to kill his Slim Shady alter ego, who’s hiding out in the spaghetti, a callback to his 2024 album, The Death of Slim Shady (Coup de Grâce), which arrived in July 2024 and topped the Billboard 200.

Eminem has reached the summit of the Billboard Hot 100 on five separate occasions in his decorated career. He reached No. 1 with both of his Rihanna collaborations “Love the Way You Lie” and “The Monster,” as well as “Not Afraid,” “Lose Yourself” and “Crack a Bottle,” the last track with his close friends Dr. Dre and 50 Cent.

50 actually revealed in October that Em is working on a new album, which caused the Detroit rapper to pull out of contributing to the Street Fighter soundtrack. Fif told the Associated Press at the film’s premiere: “Em, he put a couple pieces together for it and decided because he got closer to his album, he kinda fell back on that because he’s preparing his presentation for the next album.”

Frank Reich: I'm the Jets' offensive coordinator, our inability to score starts with me

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

The Jets haven’t scored a touchdown since the third quarter of their Week 8 loss to the Raiders. It’s now Week 11, and Jets offensive coordinator Frank Reich knows it’s past time for him to find a way to get his team into the end zone.

Reich said today that he believes the Jets have enough talent on offense to produce, and he and the coaching staff need to make it happen.

“I’m the offensive coordinator, it starts with me,” Reich said. “I, and we as a staff, have to put the players in position to do what they do. We’ve got a very talented group, so we need to do a better job coaching.”

Over the last nine quarters of play, the Jets have nine Jason Sanders field goals, and no other points. Reich is well aware that three points a quarter isn’t going to win many games.

“Obviously, players need to do their part and execute and make plays,” Reich said. “For all of us, it’s a collective effort. We know we need to put more points on the board, whether it’s in the red zone or whether it’s further out, we still want to be explosive. We need to get back on track there. It’s been nine quarters. Believe me, I feel every play of that nine quarters.”
